English
The Jewel Garland of Synonyms
Catalog
Toh 4454 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 4454 of the Derge edition) · Derge Tengyur · Miscellaneous (sna tshogs) · Medicine, Craft & Sciences

About this text

The Jewel Garland of Synonyms, in Which a Single Word Applies to Many Meanings, known in Tibetan as Skad dod gcig gis don du mar 'jug pa'i mngon brjod nor bu'i phreng ba, is a Sanskrit lexicon of homonyms, a dictionary of words that each carry several meanings, composed in verse in the tradition of Indian works on many-meaning words. After a homage to the Buddha, it sets out its entries in the order of the Sanskrit alphabet, giving for each word, such as kaḥ, which may mean Brahmā, wind, the sun or fire, the full range of senses it can bear, and it closes with a long list of indeclinable particles and verbal prefixes. It is Toh 4454 in the Tengyur, where it belongs to the section on language and lexicography, the treatises that served Tibetan translators as tools for reading Sanskrit. According to its colophon, the Indian work was copied in Nepal and later rendered into Tibetan and edited by the Zhalu translator Dharmapālabhadra, who drew on earlier translations such as the Amarakośa and kept Sanskrit words where no Tibetan equivalent could be found.

Translation

Herein is The Jewel Garland of Synonyms, in Which a Single Word Applies to Many Meanings.

Homage to the Buddha.

Following the order of the vowels and of ka and the rest,
the words ending in ka and the rest are set out;
the letter may also stand in the second syllable.
In definite order, beginning with ka:

kaḥ means Brahmā, wind, the sun,
fire, Yama himself,
light, and going.
kai means the head, water, and happiness.
The word ku means the earth and what is bad.
aka means suffering and sin.
arka means a line, night,
a sign, a characteristic,
a section of a dance and the like, a chapter,
the lap, and one who has form.
antaka means a tangled line,
a mantra, a place, and ornaments.
arka means the sun, the leaf of the arka plant,
Śakra (Indra), crystal, and copper.
eka means chief, a friend,
and whatever is not the other.
śaṃkā means a kind of bird,
Antaka (Death), a door panel, and the twice-born.
karka means the jewel karketa,
fire, a white horse, a mirror, and a vase.
kalka means sin and excrement;
kiṣṭa means a flaw and deceit,
likewise the dregs of sesame oil,
evil intent, and batura.
kāka means a crow, a seat, a snake,
the head, the face, what is bad,
pride, an island, and a tree.
kākā means crow's shank,
a crow, a row, and the crow tree.
kāka also means the peacock and the destroyer of stains;
it should be looked for under the rocky one as well.
kākaṃ means a flock of crows,
and also what binds deceit.
kuṣku means a span and a cubit,
a storehouse, and contempt.
koka means the wheel bird, the wolf,
a chief, the date palm,
the frog, and Viṣṇu.
cchaka means attachment to the home,
various wild animals, birds,
a city, and what is crooked.
ṭika means a tool that breaks stone,
a stone wall as a synonym,
pride, a particular, a monkey's dwelling,
the thigh, the scabbard of a sword,
anger, and digging.
tarka means doubt and conceptual thought,
the snake, action, and the divisions of the śātri.
tośaṃ means a line of sons and a son,
a line, and the three supports.
trikā means the rim of a well,
that which has a second, and the crow's store.
nāka means heaven and the sky.
nāku means an anthill, the hare,
and a particular kind of sage.
niṣka means gold, a coin, a measure of weight,
a hundred and eight pieces of gold,
a breast ornament, and a measure of gold.
nyadku means a deer and a kind of sage.
paṅka means mud and sin.
pāka means ripening and a child.
bāka means old, fully ripened,
the very end, and weariness.
baga means Antaka (Death), what is calm,
a kind of rākṣasa, and Kubera.
baṅga means carrying off water and the like,
baryāna, a share,
and, as an adjective, one who is defeated.
paṅka also means bark, husk, and pebble.
dhūka means a hole and time.
bheka means the frog and the cloud.
muṣka means the scrotum, a heap,
one who is liberated, and one with branches.
rūka means speechless and inferior.
raṅka means a horse and a wall.
rākā means a girl who has seen her first flow,
a river,
the full and complete moon,
and it should also be looked for under the itching disease.
reka means narrow.
parika means last, and it is adjectival.
rokai means a hole, and taking back after giving.
A mountain, ka, and beauty.
laṅgā means the city of the rākṣasas, a branch,
kulaṣṭa, and śākidī.
āloka is said to mean light.
loka means the world and people.
śaṃkā means fear and conceptual thought.
śaṃku means a particular number,
what is calm, a peg, a foot,
and also a particular weapon.
śalke means scales and bark split off.
śāka means power, a tree, an ox,
and a particular lord of men.
śāśaṃ means green leaves,
flowers, fruits, and the like.
śuka means the scatterer, the son of Vyāsa,
the one who echoes sounds, and praise.
śukaṅ means grajreparṇa,
śirīṣa, and the red tree.
śulke means a toll and the like,
and whatever is the price paid for a mother.
śrika; śuṅga means affection.
śorka means a flock of parrots,
and also a particular act of women.
ślauka means verse and fame.
sreka means wind and having blue lotuses.
stoka means small and the cātaka bird that waits for rain.
anukā means few and learned.
raṇarka means battle and affliction.
aṇaka means lineage and character.
abyuka means the sprouting of iron garlic.
adhiki means the sun and the hearth.
arñikā means frost and medicine.
aṇḍizhukā is also explained as grass,
and is well known as the elder sister.
adīka means fearless,
one who drools, one full of desire, and a poet;
for these it is adjectival.
ambikā means one with water,
and also those born among people.
White mustard seed and kyetaba,
and also for what is accomplished, asmṛṅuka.
ambikā means cukrika,
sour, likewise tibyeḍikā,
and should also be looked for under the group of sour things.
abhauka means a fish and a child,
a fool, byuṇa, and also a wall.
alaka means the city of Kubera,
a lock of hair, and powder: alaka.
alana means the white sun,
a yogin, an instigator, and a dog.
alilduka means a lie,
one who is unfriendly, and also the daytime.
aśoka means taking what is soft,
pride, and a tree.
aśokai means granting the supreme.
aśokā means heat,
rohibhā, and śokabyaka.
ādhaka means a kind of pride,
instruction, and orjeka.
ātaṅga means sickness and torment,
saṅgā, and the sound of a clay drum.
ānaka means a small drum,
a drum, a round drum, and the sound of thunder.
āloka means looking,
clarity, and speaking plainly.
āsnaśaṃ means the daytime,
the renunciant, abandoning, and a constant duty.
ikṣuku means sugarcane,
a servant, food, and the kings of the solar lineage.
udarka means a seeker,
the fruit of the black plant, and intoxicating liquor.
uphruka means an owl,
a plant, and quarreling with harsh sounds.
uṣṭaka means scorching,
mere heat, what hastens,
and the learned.
uṣṭikā means clay, a cow elephant, and an intoxicating rock.
urmikā means a wave,
a finger ring, the buzzing of bees,
a worn-out garment, and turmoil above.
saṃśukai means fine cloth,
any cloth, and an upper garment.
kañcuka means armor,
a covering that is cast off and shed,
and a garment of the body that gives delight.
kañcukī means a particular medicine.

a mountain slope, a royal palace,
a bracelet, sea salt,
an ornament, and an elephant's tusk.
kaṇaka means a hot red thing,
a hot fire, and molasses.
kabyeka means bad character,

kālikā means explanation,
going far, the real thing, and the churning of fire.
kaṇḍaka means a limb of a tree,
a place of the body, and faults,
body hair and trifles;
it should also be looked for under the killer.
kanaka means gold and the refiner of gold,
nāgagesar, caspaka,
kiśuka, a golden spoke,
and some also hold it to be the koli fruit.
kāraka means a skull cup,
a ewer, a sky-goer,
a certain shrub, the pomegranate, and the hand;
the stone of the clouds, hail, is karakā.
karaṃka means a crop,
truth, the shell of the nārikela,
and heads. kartekā means
an earring, an islet, a goad and whip,

and the seed pod of the lotus;
some also call it kuṭani.
kavaṃka means a mark,
black, a flaw, and slander.
sāṭhaka means the ruddy goose,
the yellow-headed one, and koka.
kāmuka is explained as the radiant one,
the aśoka tree, and the atimukta.
kāraka is to be known as the agent;
the first of an action is held to be the agent.
kārikā means verse,
and an extensive commentary composed upon it.
karikā means a dance and a cow elephant,
nāsita and the like, and an artisan.
karmukaṃ means bamboo and a bow;
as an adjective, one capable in action.
kālikā means a fetter,
a particular yoginī,
setting, price,
a particular crooked tree, batobyel,
a line of body hair, the smoky one,
and ink. kātī means
sin, the winged one, the cloud-strong one,
power, the divisions of smoke,
and the cloud ground; it is adjectival.
kipāka means the great black fruit
and a fool. tīcaka means
the resounding one, bamboo, an asura, wind,

tīṭaka means a kind of insect;

kulaka means the chief of a lineage,
an anthill, a crow, and what is very ka.
kulake means verses joined together,

kulika means a particular nāga,

kuśika means a kind of sage,

kuśaka means one who torments others,

kucikā means a particular tip,

tūlikyu. kuñcika means kuṭmala.
kyupaka means the mast,

kukurjara means one who has died.
kabikā is a stone in water.
An anthill and a stupa are kulaka.
kulaśaṃ means a shore.
Plowing a field with the plowshare is kṛṣaka.
kiñcaka means a little,
and the blood of another's child.
koraśaṃ means an opening bud, a skilled one,

What is called kakṣupekṣuka means steady, the limit of purity, one who tends a garden, washing, caṅgā, a livelihood, and guarding the gate. What is called kṛmikaṇḍakaṃ means a drawing, reviling, and viḍaṅga. What is called śojagariko means one who speaks blessings, and one who tends the hearth. What is called cilimbilikā means a particular creature with a neck, the light of the sky, and lightning. What is called jalakuñjaka means a turret, a water house, and praśī. What is called jalatāyika means illīśa and kākāci, which are fish, and it is also held to mean a medicine. What is called nabakaraṅka means the fruit of the cane, and a cloud. What is called nabaphalikā means a conch, a raft, a boat, the newly born, and a woman's first fresh flow. What is called nāgapārikaṃ means an elephant born of the king's she-elephant, the garuḍa, the place of a digit, and a figured girdle. What is called byabahārikā means what is to be purified, vermilion, and worldly convention. What is called brīhirājika means krāmanī and foreign rice. śatasarbikā means dūrbā grass. The word śītacambaka means the satisfier of Yama, and a lamp. sugasantakaṃ means spring, and also comes to mean one who utters proud speech. hemapuṣpeka and what is called hemapuṣpaka mean a heap of campaka flowers. grāmamaṅkurikā means a turret, and is also seen to mean a village brawl. maṅanaṇālākā means a snake, and the arising of desire. mātulasutraka means one born of a maternal uncle, and the thorn-apple fruit. latāmarkāṭaka means a girl, a flower, and a monkey. barṇabiloḍaka means one who steals the shadow of verses, and a thief. sindūratilaka means a nāga, and a mark of sindūra. snātācekitsaka means the observance of the fourth month. snātacikitsakaṃ means austerity, a woman, and a flower. Thus, from the Viśvalocana, also called the Muktāvalī, composed by the master poet, the paṇḍita, the glorious Śrīdharasena: the section of words ending in ka, with ka and its vowels.

vyavahāra means conventional usage, and also a kind of tree, and a place. śatapatra means the split one's swoon, the one possessed of wood, a pot, and the crested one. śatapatrī means one like a victor. śatāparī means the mistress of good conduct. śilumāra means the water monkey, the self-rescuer, and the Seizer. samudrāru means binding a bridge, the grasper, and the britimiṃgi. saṃprahāra means going and walking. sahacarī means jhiṇṭā. sahacara means youth and acting separately. sālasāra means a tree, and asafoetida. sukumāra means soft. sūtradhāra means a carpenter, a craftsman, anantra, the stomach, dance, a kind of vessel, and Śakra (Indra). sthiradaṃṣṭra means one who goes on its hands, and a lion that resembles a boar in form. utpalabanī means a mass of utpalas, equal, a heavenly dancer, the follower of Kapila, and a kind of non-Buddhist. tāmālābatri means a tilaka mark, tāpañci, and the leafy one. tālāśipatraṃ means tāmalakī and tīlāpa. pādacaudvara means the pippala, karakara, and śaikrta. It is also held to mean one who makes plain only the faults of others, one who strives at that, and a man. pītakāparaṃ means the bile-holder and saffron. pāśucāmara means removing dust and high praise. rasakepara means bakula, vedhaka, and the flowers of the nāga tree. rājapadāraṃ means the fruit of the lapalli, and the red emblic myrobalan. romakesara is held to mean bristling hair. basvokasmarā means the Padmaka of Śrīda (Kubera), and the city Alakāvatī. pipratīsāra means regret, anger, and latent tendency. samabhihāra means having the definite sense of again and again. sarvatobhadra means a figure of sound in poetry, and a kind of cave. sarvotobhadra means the low, food, and a woman dancer. Thus ends the section of words ending in repha, the letter ra, from the Viśvaprakāśa, otherwise named the Garland of Pearls.

The forms that have arisen as the outcome of irregular words
through adding aṇ and the others, the suffix ta, guṇa and the rest, should be examined;
the groups of genders not stated are also to be inferred;
and if some have not been included here, it is for fear of prolixity.

In the Viśvaprakāśa, also called the Garland of Pearls, the sections ending in the letter kṣa are complete.

Now, in that same proper order,
beginning with the letter ā,
in this section on various meanings,
I shall also state the indeclinables.
ā means Śrīkaṇṭha (Śiva), āmsaṃpo,
Smara (Kāma), pervading,
near, an element, the sense of remainder,
torment, and anger;
these are not to be joined in sandhi.
i means desire, weariness,
Śrī, suffering, real anger,
Śiva, the perfect Buddha, and speech.
ā means protection, the protector,
purification, an act, beginning, and countless.
ṛ means the reviling speech of the gods.
ṛ means the reviling speech of the asura women.
'ṛ also means the mother of the gods.
e means the four hands,
Smara, calling, and songs.
i is explained as Śiva,
and also Smara, calling, and competing.
aṃ means Brahmā, light, and competing.
au means the sky, calling, competing,
the supreme Dharma, and assent.
aḥ is explained as a broom.
ku means reviling, and the sense of little.
kiṃ means distinction, obstruction,
and also scolding and reviling.
ki means the future, and fatigue.
aṅga is also seen as calling to all,
joy, and the sense of again.
kiñja means beginning, and all.
ca means continuity, ascertainment,
filling out a verse line, a gloss,
gathering from everything, collecting incidentally,
the sense of mutual, focusing,
the direct, the sense of the unseen,
the crooked, the sky-goer, and the like.
nanuca means a question,
a god, reasoning, what is truly firm,
and high praise.
prañca means the best, the past, before,
dawn, and an interval,
a direction, place and time, heruka,
the middle, and binartha.
na means absence, negation,
the contrary, and what is other than that,
alike, the sense of little,
the sense of intrinsic nature, and sequence.
puṣbū means high praise,
the sense of very, good,
excellent, and blameless.
ati means between, lacking,
the sense of middle, depending,
sameness itself, abandoning now,
excellent, and above.
atas means indicating by reference,
definite teaching, the sense of the fifth case, and the agent.
antatas means teaching,
the sense of the fifth case, a limb of meditation,
and also recognition, slander, and harm.
aho means wonder, desire,
weariness, the perfect Buddha,
and compassion.
ā means oneself, far, and near.
itas is explained as certainty, parts,
and also the sense of the fifth case.
ati means a cause, completion,
an introduction, distinguishing, clarifying, and the like,
drawing along after,
kinds, the sense of "but," and conception.
u means asking, and the sense of alternatives.
ki means question and desire.
kimuta is known as excellent,
and also a question and the sense of alternatives.
kutas is held to mean realizing, asking,
and also the sense of the fifth case.
he means the sense of "you," and the sense of giving.
me means the sense of "I," and "mine."
tu means filling out a verse line,
distinction, focusing, and gathering all.
tatas means first, asking thoroughly,
the sense of the fifth case, and as in a discourse.

tāvat means unbroken and broken,
emphasis, and a little,
and complete settling.
paścāt means
the west, and also later.
purastāt means the east, and before,
the sense of former, and first.
prati means giving back, and first,
holding each one distinctly,
the arising of the principal, and the wish to obtain,
characteristic and the like, and application,
the sense of "merely," and facing toward,
clear manifestation, and speaking as well.
bata means weariness, and play, and censure,
satisfaction, calling, and wonder.
The word yatas means certainty,
the sense of the fifth case, and distinguishing.
yadvā tatri means alternatives.
yata means self, and restriction.
sīman is a section, kata.
śaśvat means again, and the sense of always.
śiti means question, and alternatives.
sakṛt means together, and once.
sākṣāt means direct perception, and equal.
svasti means aspiration, virtue, merit,
well-being, happiness, and the like.
hanta means joy, and compassion,
purity, beginning, poison, and the like;
and also in the good sense of poison and the like
the word hanta is applied.
atha and atho mean virtue,
question, beginning, doubt,
and without interruption. anyathā
means not so, and the sense of another manner.
tathā means like that, definite teaching,
certainty, and gathering all,
continuity, and producing,
summary teaching, and the individual.
yathā means inference, like that,
definite teaching, and summary teaching,
and also cause and producer.
vṛthā means sickness, and discarded,
without cause, barren, and in every case;
by that cause it should be seen in these.
ut means principal, clear manifestation,
liberation, binding, and the action of above.
byuru means obtaining, entity,
separation, one's own strength, and powerless.
anu means sequence, inferior,
the sense of after, the sense of together,
great breadth, the sense of near,
similarity, characteristic, and the like.
kinnu means question, and alternatives.
nanu means question, emphasis,
calling, drawing along after, and permission.
nānā means many, the sense of discarding,
and also the sense of both.
nina means always, certainty,
wonder, arrangement, summary, aggregate,
inner entity, and entity,
seeing, and giver,
binding, supreme peace,
liberation, virtue, doubt,
and abode. nu means the sense of drawing along after,
abode, kind, similarity,
and should be seen in the sense of cause.
apa means the Dark One, the sense of separation,
the sense of discarding, reading,
separation, the unpleasant, thief, joy,
and definite teaching.
api means possibility, need,
question, censure, gathering all,
reasoning, the meaning of a word, acting at will,
and actions. upa means
inferior, bad, pervading,
beginning, and worship,
doing without conduct, giving,
a recipient of offerings, and the past.
ut means effort, fault, and talk,
the sense of the deathless, the sense of effort,
gathering, and the wish to anoint,
and the sense of a border: so is the word well known.
The word va becomes simile;
for Varuṇa too it is this very word.
vā means alternative, and simile,
the sense of "only," and gathering all.
vai means filling out a verse line,
connection, drawing along after, and certainty.
abhi means having become thus, and talk,
strong desire, and facing toward.
abhīkṣṇam means again and again, quickly,
excellence, and continuity.
ābhīkṣṇyam likewise
is held to mean again and again, and continuity.
amā means the sense of together, near,
the new moon, and together.
apavye means effort, and permanent.
alam means ornament, the word of sufficiency,
fruitless, capable, obtaining, and emphasis.
id and the rest are for the ornament of speech.
sam is also held to mean the sense of former.
i means suffering, meditation,
anger, direct perception, and grasping.
um means question, assent, and anger.
um means question, assent, anger, and weariness.
evam means manner, simile,
acceptance, and restriction.
oṃ means assent,
speaking, high praise, and sequence.
ka means head, happiness, and water.
kathi means question, and manner.
paṃ means moving, and arising.
mami means assent.
prana means wish, following after, and the sun.
kaṃ means question, and censure.
joṣam means silence, happiness,
high praise, and moving.
uddinam means during the day,
and the sun abiding day by day.
tūṣṇīkā means merely not speaking.
tūṣṇīkam means one who is a sage.
nāma means clear manifestation, arising,
bad, acceptance, and anger.
nūnam is explained as alternative
and certainty of meaning.
prādhva means bent, conforming,
excellence, certainty, and the sense of "very."
śam means virtue, and happiness.
sam means the sense of attachment, the sense of sameness,
the sense of beauty, and the sense of excellence.
sma means the past, and filling out a verse line.
samā means censure, and having a pair.
sāmpratam means the present.
ham means an angry word, drawing along after, and body.
praguṅa means alternatives.
hūṃ means subduing, and assent;
some also hold it to mean wrongdoing.
ayikā means a portion, and bad,
speaking, understanding, a joyful word,
setting out to the battlefront, drawing along after,
going, circling round, and anger.
āya means memory, poison and the like,
the end of time, and the middle.
antarā means the sense of separation,
the sense of middle, and the sense of near.
antar means the sense of furthest end, the sense of middle,
and should be seen in the sense of one's own activity.
urarī and ūrarī
and ūrī mean vast, and made one's own.
dur means negation, hardship,
the sense of going and the like, and not excellent.
nir means negation without remainder,
and certainty in senses such as "intensely."
parā means understanding, harm,
replying, principal, supreme,
facing toward, liberation,
the sense of certainty, and subduing.
pari means all around, entity,
the wish to obtain, characteristic and the like,
embracing, clothing, pervading,
sickness, grief, worship, supreme,
protector of the earth, speaking of faults, and discarding.
punar means distinction, first,
having become former, ancient times,
being firmly bound, the past,
above the earth, the higher realms, and the other world.
kila means beauty, history,
possibility, and the sense of drawing along after.
khalu is remembered for the ornament of speech, the wish to obtain, negation,
certainty, continuity, not speaking,
the wish to know, and the like.
ava means pervading, thorough meditation,
separation, object, negation,
the sense of a little, consciousness,
simile, and restriction.
vas means the sense of "you know,"
division, and manner,
the past, the sense of distinct, principal,
entity, and sky-goers.
uṣā means number, having number,
the end of night, and the wish to lie down to sleep.
doṣā means the door of night,
night, and also cloud.
niṣaṣā means end and middle,
protection, and also other.
agrahas means first,
tip, supreme, that very thing,
and the silent.

abhitas means end, and all ends, facing toward,
both, and quick.
tiras means animal, invisible,
weariness, and negation,
entirety, and the past.
nīcais is held to mean having little independence.
puras means supreme, and first.
guraṅsana means first and supreme.
prātar means the early part of the day, the morning.
pūrvedyus means the ground of the Dharma,
the sense of former, and the day before,
after and after, and again and again,
in the sense of many. mithas
means mutual, and seizing.
prādus means clear manifestation, conduct,
and should be seen also for mere existence.
śanais is explained as moving slowly,
independence, and good worship,
the sense of certainty, and following after,
hardship, and genuine prosperity,
just at that time, and haphazard,
and also what occurs suddenly.
mahā means born of grief,
bad, poison, and the sense of compassion.
aho is held to mean question, discernment,
wonder, and protecting.
ahāhā means that wonder arises.
ahaha means latent tendencies,
total affliction, and great joy.
āha means moving, and the sense of definite application.
utāho means considering these.
The word saha means all,
at once, and genuine joining,
similar, existing, and connection.
ha means filling out a verse line,
connection, and inferior.
hā is for poison and the like.
hāhā is well known for weariness;
for a gandharva too there is Hāhā.
he is for distinction, restriction,
and filling out a verse line.
hī is for wonder, poison and the like,
joy, and the causes of suffering.
hītī means wonder, and laughter.
hūtū means joy, and a gandharva.
he and hai mean not arriving, remembering,
calling, and causes.
ho and ho are held to mean all,
calling, and rivalry.
mikṣa means quick, and the sense of certainty;
some also hold it to mean that very thing.

These are the inexhaustible words having many meanings.

In the Nepalese year three hundred and eighty-one, on the eighth day of the dark half of the month of Phālguna, on the day of Jupiter, Thursday, in the town of Śrī Lalitapura, at the great monastery where Gautama especially abides, the section that the paṇḍita and elder Glorious Supreme Lineage Śrījñāna had written down is complete.

This was done at the command of the supreme and peerless object of offerings for gods and humans, the Precious Nirmāṇakāya, the Fourth Holder of the Red Crown, the Precious Chennga of Thel, the precious seat of the glorious Phakmo Drupa. The one called Zhalu Lotsāwa, the monk Dharmapālabhadra, relying on earlier translations such as the Treasury of the Immortal (Amarakośa), left out such things as the words that identify grammatical markers, which are of no use in Tibetan; he kept the Sanskrit as it was where it was needed as the basis of explanation, and left as they were those for which no equivalent was found. Thus he translated it, edited it, and made it correct.

Translator's notes

This is a Sanskrit homonym lexicon: each Sanskrit headword, written in Tibetan letters, is followed by the meanings it can bear. The headwords are given exactly as the Tibetan spells them, and many are evidently corrupt in transmission; meanings are translated from the Tibetan glosses, several of which are themselves garbled. Doubtful points: "light" reads གསའ་བ as གསལ་བ; "a particular number" for śaṃku reads གངས as གྲངས; "molasses" for kaṇaka reads བྱོ་འ as བུ་རམ only conjecturally; the gloss "a girl who has seen her first flow" for rākā interprets a corrupt phrase; the isolated line "A mountain, ka, and beauty" and the glosses for tarka (the divisions of the śātri), tośaṃ, abyuka and kañcuka are uncertain. ལུས་ངན is taken as Kubera, whose city is Alakā. As in the previous piece, the Sanskrit headwords are given exactly as the Tibetan spells them, and both headwords and glosses are often corrupt; many glosses are rendered literally where their intended sense cannot be recovered with confidence. Identifications that rest on the evident Sanskrit behind the Tibetan include the atimukta creeper (Tibetan "very freed"), Viśvāmitra (Tibetan "various friend of the senses"), the mast (Tibetan "tree of qualities," guṇavṛkṣa), the leather-winged one as the bat, the lord of the west as Varuṇa, and the perfumed chamber (gandhakuṭī). "A certain shrub" renders the unidentified Tibetan plant name le brgan. In the karmukaṃ entry a stray syllable kri is disregarded. As before, Sanskrit headwords are reproduced as the Tibetan spells them; both headwords and glosses are frequently corrupt, and many glosses are rendered literally. Garlok (Tibetan gar log) is the Tibetan name for a Turkic people, here glossing turuṣka. The stray syllable thu before "nāga" in the takṣaka entry is disregarded. Sweetwood renders Tibetan shing mngar, licorice. "The ragan" renders an unidentified Tibetan word (ra gan, possibly brass). This is a lexicon of Sanskrit homonyms; headwords are romanized exactly as the Tibetan spells them, even where the standard Sanskrit form differs (e.g. rucarka for rucaka, betarka for vitarka). Several glosses rest on probable copyists' slips: "ya mang agna" read as Jamadagni (Reṇukā is his wife); "khyem" read as khyim, "house, household" (rātrika, ṅañcaka, svasteka); "khye" read as khyi, "dog" (sūcaka); "rmod pa" read as smod pa, "reviling" (ākṣepaka); "dag dad" read as ngag dang, "speech and" (eḍamūka, deaf and dumb). Some headwords are evidently miswritten and are given in their evident form: garḍaddhā as gardabhī, brāma as grāma, and bhāma (the second one) as bhrama.
